TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

In a famous scene from Band of Brothers , the HBO epic about a U.S. paratrooper regiment in World War II, a Nazi general surrenders to U.S. forces. The Americans, rather than humiliating him, allow him to deliver a stirring address to his troops after they lay down their arms; the soldiers from both camps are visibly moved. That incident may be exaggerated or apocryphal, but it echoed a widely held sentiment among the Allies in 1945: “In victory, magnanimity,” as Winston Churchill once summarized. But over four decades later, as the Berlin Wall fell and the Soviet Union collapsed, magnanimity was harder to come by: How much, if anything, was the United States willing to offer Russia in the wake of the Cold War? That question is at the heart of two books, Hubris: The American Origins of Russia’s War Against Ukraine by British historian Jonathan Haslam and Boiling Point: How America Brought War to Ukraine by journalist Stephen Kinzer. Both authors question to what extent Russia’s war in Ukraine is a reaction to decades of arrogance by the United States, full of swagger as the last superpower standing amid the ashes of the Cold War. Hubris: The American Origins of Russia’s War Against Ukraine , Jonathan Haslam, Belknap Press, 368 pp., $25.95, September 2026, paperback Both accounts, though overeager to blame Washington for Moscow’s belligerent turn, serve as a helpful reminder of the paths not taken in the 1990s and early 2000s, when, rather than bringing Russia into the liberal world order as a true partner, the United States encouraged rapid NATO expansion and democratization in Eastern and Central Europe, antagonizing the country. * AI Isn’t a Revolution—Yet
  Foreign Policy | 30 Oct 19:00 UTC
  Just as British people did with steam power, the American public can shape how this technology spreads.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For a time, in the 1980s, the Industrial Revolution lost its status as a “revolution.” New quantitative measures threatened to demote Britain’s 18th-century economic transformation to the status of an unglamorous, long-term “process.” It simply unfolded too slowly and in too few sectors to justify terminology implying total, overnight transformation. Ultimately, the Industrial Revolution was rehabilitated as a “revolution”—albeit with the recognition that it was more gradual and uneven than the word traditionally implies. The question in this scholarly debate was never whether the steam engine was revolutionary, because people and institutions determine whether a potentially game-changing invention turns out to have truly revolutionary impact. A key factor in the Industrial Revolution’s slow unfolding was British people’s active resistance to changes instigated by steam power and factory organization—to an extent that shook posterity’s very certainty of its standing as a “revolution.” For a time, in the 1980s, the Industrial Revolution lost its status as a “revolution.” New quantitative measures threatened to demote Britain’s 18th-century economic transformation to the status of an unglamorous, long-term “process.” It simply unfolded too slowly and in too few sectors to justify terminology implying total, overnight transformation. Before his death, Markovic wrote to his brother saying that, if anything were to happen to him, Delon would be “10,000% responsible.” The scandal following both Markovic’s death centered on this murky and complex relationship. A PR blast announced “Netflix presents The New Stanford Prison Experiment .” Was this an attempt to one-up the satirically dystopian phrases in David Foster Wallace’s Infinite Jest ? A “yes, and” to Wallace’s “Year of the Depend Adult Undergarment”? Alas, no. And for the streaming service that only became more ubiquitous after the global success of the gleefully sadistic Squid Game , it seemed apt. The phrase “Stanford prison experiment,” like Lord of the Flies , is pretty much synonymous with violence, anarchy, and the bleakest confirmation of man’s inhumanity to man. Do we really need a “new” one? Chances are you know about the original Stanford prison experiment. In August 1971, with the campus on break, a psychology professor at Stanford University named Philip Zimbardo offered a paying gig to students willing to run through an unusual and morally questionable exercise. The basement of a university building was made up to resemble a prison. Twenty-four students (all male, all white except for one Asian-American; “good boys,” Zimbardo called them) were randomly split into groups of inmates or guards and given appropriate uniforms. For two weeks, the professor thought, he would take notes on how unchecked power in an enclosed system would reveal itself. The experiment, funded by the U.S. Office of Naval Research, ended after six days, and that’s only because Zimbardo’s girlfriend stopped by and said, “Holy crap, what the hell is happening here?!” Stanford professor Philip Zimbardo discusses the Stanford prison experiment on Aug. 20, 1971. Duke Downey/San Francisco Chronicle via Getty Images At least that’s the lore. Roosevelt had entered office ready to work with established power as he launched his reflationary, state-expanding New Deal. Yet the president had grown increasingly frustrated by the obstructiveness of the Supreme Court—which had blocked programs such as the National Recovery Administration, designed to end “cut-throat” competition—and by the hostility of newspapers and tycoons. His enemies insinuated that he was a communist , and a would-be dictator . He also faced the rise of the radical right, which sought to exploit the public’s fear and frustration. Roosevelt realized that by facing down the “economic royalists,” as he put it in his acceptance speech at the 1936 Democratic National Convention, he could cast himself as the people’s tribune, uniting the disparate groups supporting him. * The Security Challenges Facing the Next Israeli Leader
  Foreign Policy | 30 Oct 16:58 UTC
  Netanyahu’s wars have weakened foes, but they’re unsustainable.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Israel’s election results remain unclear , but whether current Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu again emerges triumphant or a new government, perhaps led by Gadi Eisenkot, takes power, the country is at a crossroads. Although Israel has demonstrated its military power throughout the Middle East, it is trapped in endless conflict on multiple fronts, overstretched at home, and damaging its long-term relationship with the United States. In the three years since Oct. 7, 2023, when a Hamas terrorist attack killed almost 1,200 Israelis, Israel’s regional military power has reached new heights. Not only did Israel smash Hamas after the attack, but it also took on the far more formidable Hezbollah, severely weakening its leadership, arsenal, and fighting forces in a continuing campaign . In 2025 and then again in 2026, Israel (along with the United States) attacked Iran, killing much of its senior leadership and setting back its nuclear program and military. In addition to striking its enemies, Israel now occupies around 70 percent of the Gaza Strip, large parts of Lebanon, and several miles along its border with Syria, using these buffer zones to guard against another Oct. 7-like attack and make it more difficult for terrorist groups to use drones, rockets, and other short-distance systems to strike Israel. * The Gulf Isn’t Ready for a Renewed Iran War
  Foreign Policy | 30 Oct 16:43 UTC
  The region’s energy infrastructure can’t absorb another round of fighting.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. By the narrow measure of barrels shipped, the Persian Gulf has weathered eight months of war better than most energy analysts expected. Oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz and alternative routes averaged more than 24 million barrels a day in the week ending Oct. 18, above the prewar level of 23.3 million, according to the ship-tracking firm Kpler. JPMorgan analysts wrote last week that energy markets are “converging on a forever conflict” and that view has encouraged a belief in the Trump administration that the region can absorb another round of fighting. That view confuses how much oil and gas are still flowing from the Gulf with the state of the plants and terminals that move it. Since the war began on Feb. 28, Iranian missiles and drones have damaged refineries, liquefied natural gas (LNG) trains, and export terminals across the Gulf, and the Houthis have opened a second front in the Red Sea. Much of what has been lost will take years to rebuild. The cost will fall mostly on importers in Asia, Europe, and the developing world that built their energy systems around reliable Gulf supply. By the narrow measure of barrels shipped, the Persian Gulf has weathered eight months of war better than most energy analysts expected. Oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz and alternative routes averaged more than 24 million barrels a day in the week ending Oct. 18, above the prewar level of 23.3 million, according to the ship-tracking firm Kpler. JPMorgan analysts wrote last week that energy markets are “converging on a forever conflict” and that view has encouraged a belief in the Trump administration that the region can absorb another round of fighting. That view confuses how much oil and gas are still flowing from the Gulf with the state of the plants and terminals that move it. Since the war began on Feb. 28, Iranian missiles and drones have damaged refineries, liquefied natural gas (LNG) trains, and export terminals across the Gulf, and the Houthis have opened a second front in the Red Sea. Much of what has been lost will take years to rebuild. The cost will fall mostly on importers in Asia, Europe, and the developing world that built their energy systems around reliable Gulf supply. U.S. and Israeli planners have drawn up options for a short, intensive campaign against Iran’s nuclear facilities, missile and drone arsenals, and energy infrastructure after the U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3, the New York Tim
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/30/iran-war-gulf-energy-infrastructure/

  Foreign Policy | 30 Oct 14:47 UTC
  The war has left its mark on the port city of Bandar Abbas.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. BANDAR ABBAS, Iran—Along Iran’s main highway south, debris lies scattered across the road, while bridges and tunnels are damaged or destroyed. Half-buried in the dirt, a broken road sign points toward Bandar Abbas, Iran’s main port city and the gateway to the Strait of Hormuz. “The war has never stopped here,” said Khaleh Zeynab, 51, a food vendor working alongside Bandar Abbas’s corniche. Half-buried in the dirt, a broken road sign points toward Bandar Abbas, Iran’s main port city and the gateway to the Strait of Hormuz. “The war has never stopped here,” said Khaleh Zeynab, 51, a food vendor working alongside Bandar Abbas’s corniche. She gestures toward the beach and the strait. “My son Reza is out there fighting. He’s been working on a ship since the beginning of the war. I know he could be killed at any moment, and that scares me, but he’s a soldier. If he doesn’t do his job, somebody else has to. We have to defend our country.” When the U.S.-Israeli attack on Iran began on Feb. 28, residents in Bandar Abbas watched ships burn, missiles streak across the sky, and fighter jets fly overhead. Months later, explosions from the strait can still be heard almost daily, though it is not always clear whether they are air defense interceptions or fresh attacks. What is clear, however, is that the war has made life increasingly difficult for communities whose livelihoods depend on the Strait of Hormuz. Khaleh Zeynab sells bread in Bandar Abbas on Sept. 19. “It’s been tough to make ends meet,” Zeynab said. Before the war, Bandar Abbas drew tourists from across Iran as well as abroad. Since the fighting began, visitors have largely disappeared, while many residents—seeking safety after repeated attacks on the city—left for smaller villages elsewhere in Hormozgan province. At the same time, prices have surged across Iran, especially in urban centers. Food costs have risen sharply, often even doubling, adding to the pressure on families already struggling to get by. * Russia’s Next Mobilization Is Already Happening
  Foreign Policy | 30 Oct 06:51 UTC
  With military recruitment falling short, the Kremlin has switched to stealth and coercion.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Understanding the conflict four years on. Nothing in almost five years of Russia’s war on Ukraine did more damage to the Kremlin’s hold on Russian public opinion than when Russian President Vladimir Putin signed a decree to forcibly mobilize 300,000 men for the war on Sept. 21, 2022. Levada’s monthly mood survey , run continuously since the 1990s, recorded the sharpest one-time collapse in its history: The share of Russians describing their general mood as “great” fell from 15 percent to 7 percent almost overnight, while those reporting fear more than tripled. The share who believed the war was “progressing successfully” dropped from 73 percent in May to 53 percent by the end of September. An estimated 300,000 to half a million men of fighting age left the country within two weeks of the decree, more than had fled during all of the war up to then. Kazakhstan alone recorded some 400,000 arrivals from Russia by late November that year. At the Verkhny Lars border crossing into Georgia, cars were lined up for days. So acute was the fallout from the 2022 mobilization drive that many concluded it was too risky for Putin to repeat—even in an authoritarian police state where citizens’ opinions matter little. Thus, the biggest question surrounding Russia’s sham parliamentary elections last month was never who would win, but whether another extremely unpopular mobilization would have to wait until after the vote.   FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Teh Eng Koon/AFP via Getty Images) A week before Election Day, a Mandarin-language ad targeting Republican U.S. Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith aired in Mississippi. The ad ends with the slogan “China First, Mississippi Last,” implying that Hyde-Smith has used her position to sell off farmland to China at Mississippi’s expense. Frowned upon by some progressive commentators as racist and xenophobic, the ad’s existence raises big questions about the future of the Democratic Party’s posture toward China. What role will China play in the party’s approach to the world? Will we see more Democrats oppose rapprochement with China or any efforts to slow the decoupling of the U.S. and Chinese economies? Will Democratic candidates join in the Republican effort to restrict immigration and investment from Chinese individuals in the name of national security? For China observers, there are some parts of the 15-second spot that signal a lack of familiarity with the realities of contemporary China. For one, the ad features subtitles in traditional Chinese characters rather than the simplified characters actually used in mainland China. (It’s also worth noting that an insistence on the continued use of traditional characters has been an anti-China culture war touch point in Hong Kong and Taiwan.) More importantly, it makes hay of Chinese purchases of U.S. farmland, which have become an increasingly salient issue among the Republican Party’s China hawks, despite a general consensus among foreign policy experts that concerns over these farmland purchases are overblown. They “do not represent a substantial enough portion of food production in the United States to threaten national food security,” in the words of Jamie Lutz and Caitlin Welsh of the Center for Strategic and International Studies. That hasn’t stopped Democrats from joining in on the GOP uproar, with a recent bill restricting Chinese purchases of U.S. agricultural land picking up support from a handful of Democratic representatives, mostly from California.   FULL TEXT: Until early September, my Facebook feed looked much as it always had. Posts from friends, relatives and people with whom I shared mutual friends revolved around everyday life: weddings and engagement celebrations, condolences for elderly people who had died and the occasional tragedy of a traffic accident. Then, on Sept. 3, Houthi fighters launched a major military offensive against government-held areas of western Yemen, seizing six districts within a week. A month later, the internationally recognized Yemeni government responded with Operation Dawn of Yemen, aimed not only at recapturing lost territory but also at driving the Houthis out of other areas under their control. As fighting intensified across the front lines, an unusual number of mourning posts began appearing in my Facebook feed. Facebook remains Yemen’s public square, a place where people mourn their dead, celebrate milestones, document their lives and argue fiercely over the country’s politics. By early October, as the Houthis advanced deeper into government-held areas of Taiz province and approached the eponymous city itself, my feed had turned into an endless stream of obituaries. Parents grieved for their sons. Wives mourned their husbands. Friends wrote heartbreaking tributes to men they had known since childhood. As I began reading and collecting these posts, Facebook’s algorithm appeared to take notice of my interest. Each time I opened the platform, more photographs of the dead appeared, many shared by people who were not even on my friends list. Scrolling through Facebook began to feel like walking past a wall covered with photographs of the dead. “Hisham, the hero, my brother and beloved martyr, please come back, you troublemaker. My heart aches from your absence,” wrote Shehab al-Shehabi, a journalist, on Facebook on Oct. 13, sharing a photograph of Hisham, who looked barely out of childhood, dressed in a shirt, with a tan tactical vest. Unlike in Houthi-controlled areas, where information about fighters’ deaths appears to be tightly controlled and casualties are often announced through official funerals, families and friends in government-controlled areas openly share photographs, names and personal stories of those killed on the battlefield, making public expressions of grief more visible and spontaneous. * In praise of the dilettante
  Aeon | 30 Oct 10:00 UTC
  A wayfaring cosmopolitan who never gets stuck, the dilettante is the right anti-hero for our hyper-specialised age - by Jay Miller Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jean Cocteau, poet, artist, filmmaker, photographed in 1948 by Philippe Halsman. Courtesy Magnum Photos Why, of all people, defend the dilettante ? An amateur at best, a flighty rascal at worst: why take an interest in someone whose own interests are never more than a passing hobby? These are serious times – why take seriously someone for whom nothing is serious? Merriam-Webster defines the dilettante as ‘a person having a superficial interest in an art or a branch of knowledge’. Encyclopedia.com calls the dilettante someone who cultivates an interest ‘without real commitment or knowledge’. Cambridge settles for someone whose understanding ‘is not very deep or serious’. Collins gives us someone who ‘seems interested in a subject … but does not really know very much about it’. None of it is flattering. Clearly, the dilettante is no aspirational character. No university markets itself as training the next generation of dilettantes; no magazine runs a ‘dilettante of the year’ issue (except, perhaps, for the excellent Idler publication). He – for the dilettante is, historically and in popular imagination, so often a ‘he’ – is in essence an amateur, a role defined by wide interests and narrow expertise. Such is the reputation of the dilettante, assigned and maintained by a culture that treats a single-minded devotion to one pursuit as the only route to a meaningful life. Pick something and stick with it, we are told. It’s not so much an explicit campaign against dilettantism I’m challenging, but rather the diffuse yet relentless pressure to narrow one’s interests, to specialise, to gain expertise, to forgo breadth for the sake of depth. We hear it in the barrage of imperatives to follow your dreams or find your passion uttered at graduation speeches, in self-help guides, and similarly corporate-sounding pep talks. Under this scheme, the specialist is the hero and the dilettante is but a cautionary tale: the person who never committed, never finished, never got good enough at any one thing. But what if the scheme has it backwards? What if the dilettante’s perpetual movement from one thing to another is not a failure of nerve but a different and defensible way of being, one whose exploratory zeal turns out to be just what a hyper-specialised, over-optimised and all-too- serious world needs most? Then the dilettante would turn out to be not a capricious buffoon but a kind of modern anti-hero. That, I argue, is a dilettantism worth defending. F irst, let’s consider
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-the-dilettante-is-the-anti-hero-of-our-over-optimised-age

  FULL TEXT: On the far eastern edge of Cairo, on Oct. 27, 2026, the New Cairo Misdemeanour Court (NCMC) was the scene of two events that will shape the future of independent journalism in Egypt. Three minutes, it turned out, were all he needed to remove one of Egypt’s most prominent independent news outlets from the scene. He fined Lina Attalah, the editor-in-chief of Mada Masr, 3 million Egyptian pounds (around $53,450) for running a website without a license, and ordered the closure of the outlet that had fended off this fate for more than 13 years, holding firm against website blocking and official harassment of its journalists. At the same time, three floors away in the same building, lawyers were gathering in another courtroom for a detention-renewal hearing for six journalists: the entire newsroom of Matsda2sh (“Don’t Believe It”), a platform that specializes in fact-checking and verifying news. Over videoconference, the Supreme State Security Prosecution (SSSP) extended their detention by 15 days on top of the time they had been held since Sept. 28, 2026, as they faced charges including joining a terrorist group and spreading false news. Together, the two cases illustrate how authorities are targeting independent media through different legal mechanisms, from licensing prosecutions to national security charges. The scenes sum up the state of independent journalism in Egypt, amid a fraught political climate and mixed signals that swing between a security clampdown and the prospect of a loosening of restrictions. A clear pattern stands out: Official rhetoric warning of threats to national security has risen in lockstep with an escalating campaign against independent media.   FULL TEXT: When I was a boy in Syria in the early 1990s, I spent my summer breaks going to work with my father. I rode out with him to the farm and helped him run it. These three small towns on the outskirts of Damascus sit next to vast stretches of agricultural land. I remember one discussion in particular that my father and the neighbors often had. They would sit in circles, and each farmer would vent his resentment at how much farmland the Hafez al-Assad regime had taken from them in 1983, when I was 1 year old, under a law that allowed the state to seize private land for public projects — a law that the new authorities in Damascus continue to uphold today, despite street protests against it. That law is now the new government’s problem, amid growing popular frustration. In the nearly two years since the Bashar al-Assad regime fell, the interim authorities have signed two large real estate deals with Saudi developers on land in the western suburbs of Damascus that the old regime took from farmers in the 1970s and 1980s: Abyat Hills in Qudsaya and Sham View between Qudsaya and Dummar. In late September, the original owners marched on both sites and stopped the machinery. The government responded that the expropriations were legal and all requisite compensation had been paid. Even for people from the area like me, the protests and the public debate since the downfall of the Assad regime have revealed the scale of the issue in ways we had not previously understood. In the towns along the Damascus-Beirut highway alone, residents count tens of thousands of acres taken for state housing under two decrees signed by Hafez al-Assad in the mid-1970s, with thousands more in Dummar. The Assad regime used the expropriation law to build military bases, security branches and loyalist housing around the Syrian capital. Much of the land the regime took is still in the state’s hands, either fenced off or built on. The new authorities have inherited that issue, but, to the farmers of my father’s generation, they have so far shown no intention of correcting a basic injustice. My own family, my father and his three brothers, lost about 50 acres spread across all three towns. They had bought the land with their hard-earned money while their widowed mother was alive. The distinction that will ultimately matter is … whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. African countries are already building AI for digital transformation, as outlined in the African Union’s continental AI strategy and country-specific AI strategies.   FULL TEXT: First discovered in Egypt in the late 19th century, the collection of clay tablets known as the Amarna letters is one of our richest windows into the ancient world. The post Serbia’s Victorious New Democrats Face an Old Question appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: For the second time in just over a quarter-century, Serbia has seemingly thrown off the yoke of one-man rule. In October 2000, Serbians mobilized — first at the ballot box, then in the streets — to oust the genocidal regime of Slobodan Milošević . In October 2026, it was sufficient for them merely to vote in overwhelming numbers to force the concession of the strongman at the helm. This time Serbians ousted Milošević’s protege, Aleksandar Vučić, and his ruling Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) after their nearly 14-year tenure in government. The vote capped two years of protest that began in November 2024, when a newly renovated canopy at Novi Sad’s railway station collapsed, killing 16 people. An insurgent student movement blamed the deaths on government corruption, which they saw as a hallmark of Vučić’s rule, and their ensuing blockades of universities grew into the largest protest movement in Serbia since 2000. The movement eventually coalesced into the so-called Student List parliamentary coalition. On Sunday, this bloc won 53% of the popular vote and secured around 140 seats in the country’s 250-seat National Assembly, providing it with a comfortable, stable governing majority. Domestically, the Student List ran on a sweeping anti-corruption platform. Its foreign policy agenda, however, is virtually indistinguishable from that of the SNS — at least on paper. Why? Because Serbian nationalism remains, arguably, the most potent political force in the country. The vote that propelled the Serbian List into power is a heterodox amalgam of the country’s moderate, liberal public and the antiregime far right (voters who believe Vučić has been insufficiently hard-line, for instance, in opposing Kosovo’s sovereignty, and would like the country to pursue still deeper ties with Moscow). The same curious combination was evident throughout the two-year protest movement preceding the vote. A very similar coalition toppled Milošević in 2000. This is the bind in which Serbia’s would-be democrats find themselves. Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. Yet the past 40 years suggest that, much as in Russia, Serbia’s regional ambitions and democratization at home are at odds. The reason is institutional. I hate titles that are unmemorable strings of nonspecific words!) Also on my Libby shelf are, among others, Lovers XXX by Allie Rowbottom, The Original by Nell Stevens, and Villa Coco by Andrew Sean Greer. On my Bookshop.org app, I have The Remembered Soldier , a magnificent novel by the Dutch writer Anjet Daanje that is very long and that I ended up (happily) buying because I didn’t want to race Libby’s loan period. * Love with nowhere to go
  Aeon | 27 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Grief is not only for the dead. When we are unable to honour other profound losses, we become strangers to ourselves - by Derek van Zoonen Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Telemark, Norway, 2011. Photo by Jonas Bendiksen/Magnum Photos I have been grieving my mother for years, even though she is still alive. Sometimes before falling asleep – in that liminal space between waking and sleeping where the self becomes porous – I’d see the image of the sweet and caring woman who would come to say goodnight, her cheeks covered in thick layers of night cream. Then, out of nowhere, the image would flip into the raging woman who was so entangled in her own suffering that she turned her frustration on us. Over the years, that second woman slowly swallowed the first, as she got pulled into my brother’s addiction. It’s hard to say where her own unhappiness ended and my brother’s suffering began, but together they drifted into a parallel reality, cut off from the world beyond their heads. There was no funeral for this. No condolence cards. No flowers. No phone calls. No leave of absence. What was I supposed to mourn? And yet what I felt for them, what I still feel, has the unmistakable gravity of mourning. Can there be grief without death? I have come to think that we grieve much more often than we realise, and that death is grief’s most visible occasion rather than its essence. If that doesn’t match your experience, it may be because we rarely call these other losses by their name. Most of our losses arrive without a funeral. That is the human predicament: we are creatures built to attach, living in a world where nothing stays. Sooner or later, we lose the things we care about. And since we are complex beings with a body, a mind, a community and an environment – beings who love many different things – there is a great deal we can lose, from our physical health to our sense of who we are, from relationships to homelands to the natural world we share with other species. For creatures like us, there is no way around heartbreak: to live is to lose. Grief is what love does when its object is gone. It is love with nowhere to go. Over the past few years I have accumulated many such losses. There is the psychological loss of my father as I knew and loved him: a brutal stroke rendered him incapable of walking and cycling, and cycling was the only thing that could ‘make his thoughts liquid’, as he used to say. There is the loss of old friendships. First you are no longer part of each other’s lives – you just update one another about your life – and then, one day, it becomes awkward to reach out at all.   FULL TEXT: In early September, images of huge queues forming outside Iraqi gas stations spread across the country like wildfire. A few dozen yards away, Mohammed S., a 44-year-old tailor, agreed: “I was in Iran last week with my family. I can assure you that the Iranian population is far less affected than we are by the current crisis,” he said. As proof, he pointed to “an economy and consumption at a standstill,” which in his case has meant his income being “cut in half” despite working longer hours. And even though his business has nothing to do with oil, he said his situation is intimately tied to that of the country. “When war threatens to resume on your doorstep, and the prices of basic necessities soar, what do you do? Well, you save, just in case. That’s what we’re all doing. … Iraqis are buying only the bare minimum now, and we are paying the price.” It was a crisis for which the authorities were, quite clearly, poorly prepared, despite how crucial oil exports are to the country. Oil revenues account for around 90% of Iraq’s public revenues and are almost the sole factor determining the balance of the state budget. So in Iraq, perhaps more than elsewhere, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz — a route through which more than 90% of the country’s 3.4 million barrels of daily crude exports used to pass — quickly created a major fiscal squeeze. This was accompanied by periods of gasoline shortages, however paradoxical that may seem. Despite Iraq’s huge production, and because of often aging refineries — which also
  https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/iraqs-fuel-crisis-exposes-its-oil-dependence/

* AI models cost more when you don’t use English
  Rest of World | 26 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Some languages require far more tokens for AI models to process, raising costs and reducing context for the people who use them.
  FULL TEXT: People who use less widely spoken languages could end up paying more to use artificial intelligence than those who speak English or other dominant languages. A new study published last month by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found AI models require far more tokens for some languages than others. Tokens are the units of text or data that the models process. The researchers have called this a hidden “tokenizer tax,” which can make AI more expensive in languages with smaller online presence. A tokenizer is a tool that breaks down raw text, images, or audio into tokens. Even within the 20 most popular languages on the internet, there is a gap in token usage, the research found. The most expensive of the languages used between 1.5 and 5 times more tokens than the cheapest. This gap widened when researchers expanded the set to 200 languages, with the token cost gap in one AI model growing by 17 times. “A startup in [Bengaluru] may pay a surcharge on every prompt and completion while its competitor in Austin does not,” the study said. “The tokenizer tax also has implications beyond cost. Higher token counts translate into higher latency and reduced effective context capacity. These penalties hit hardest where compute and connectivity are already limited. Tokenizer inequity is a constraint on who can participate in the AI economy.” Non-English languages leave less room for documents or conversation history, and require more work to generate the answer, according to Pratyush Choudhury, co-founder of Activate AI, India’s first venture capital fund dedicated exclusively to AI. Activate backs multilingual voice AI startups like ElevenLabs, Sarvam, and Wispr Flow. “Think about a tutor: Retaining the student’s earlier mistakes and explanations is part of delivering a good lesson. If that history consumes the context window much faster in one language, the disadvantage reaches into the quality of the service,” he said. One way to improve language processing is to improve the underlying data. “Open data efforts matter here, like RESPIN, a collaborative initiative with [the Indian Institute of Science] funded by the Gates Foundation, where we collected text from native speakers of 38 dialects along with read speech,” Raoul Nanavati, co-founder and CEO of Mumbai-based AI voice infrastructure startup Navana.ai , told Rest of World . However, the study by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found the rarity of a language isn’t the only determina
  https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-tokenizer-tax-non-english-languages-cost-inequity/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* How to build real peace
  Aeon | 26 Oct 10:00 UTC
  From Colombia to Syria, I’ve seen that capitalist logic can’t help to rebuild postwar countries but cooperatives can - by Azize Aslan Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In Rojava, a formerly autonomous region in northeastern Syria, August 2017. Photo by Jade Sacker/NurPhoto/Getty Images When I was seven years old, my family was forced to flee escalating violence. We left Bakur – a Kurdish region in what is currently southeastern Turkey – for a city further west, where we worked in Turkish fields, living in a tent until we could save up to rent a house on the city margins, in a neighbourhood with no electricity or road access. Those are some of my earliest memories of everyday violence against people like me. For centuries, my ancestors had lived in relative harmony with other communities. But, with the emergence of modern nation-states in the region, Kurdistan – the traditional homeland of the Kurds – was fractured into southeastern Turkey, northern Iraq, northwestern Iran, and northeastern Syria. A violent assimilation was imposed upon us. For more than a century, Kurdish people have been denied recognition as a nation, their language banned, erased from maps, stripped of citizenship rights, and dispossessed of their lands. For my generation, therefore, freedom was never an abstract political matter; armed struggle was often seen as a necessity to achieve it. Even so, national liberation movements always sought dialogue with the states they fought against. I grew up inside that insistence, carrying the hope of a dignified peace. In 2013, after almost three decades of war, peace talks were held between the Turkish state and the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK), which first sought an independent Kurdish state and, later, autonomy and increased political and cultural rights for Kurds within Turkey. In 2015, these collapsed. War returned to Kurdistan and, with it, the persecution of everyone who had staked something on that possibility of peace. This included me. I was a doctoral student at the time, researching economic autonomy in Kurdistan, and it became clear that this work would no longer be tenable in the new political climate. I moved to Latin America, to complete my doctoral studies and study revolutionary movements in that part of the world, grappling all the while with a question that had become inseparable from my own existence: how could peace be built in Kurdistan? T he academic literature on peace told me I might find an answer in Guatemala.   https://aeon.co/essays/what-cooperatives-for-an-anticapitalist-peace-look-like
